And then I’m distracted all day, even at work, which is not helpful when someone’s left muddy footprints through the hallway again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I see the number the same way other people do. They say I’m too thin, but in my mind, if I stay small and… tidy-looking, I guess, it feels like I’m keeping things under control.\n\nBut then if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full, I get this awful feeling like I’ve let myself go. Like I’ve failed somehow. Which sounds dramatic, I know. It’s just—when you’re cleaning all day, you notice every little smudge and bit of dust, and I think I do that with myself too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight or body shape every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think. It sort of sneaks in while I’m doing other things. Like I’ll be wiping down a sink or straightening a trash liner and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether my waistband feels different.\n\nWhen I stay small and neat, it feels like I’m handling everything the right way. But if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just… I feel like I’ve let myself go. Even if nobody else would notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what would count as excessive, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel like I ate too much even if it’s just… more than I planned, or something that feels heavy.\n\nBut a large amount in a short time? I don’t know. I don’t think of it that way. I tend to remember more how it feels afterward, like whether I feel too full or uncomfortable. And then I get distracted thinking about it, like when I’m mopping and I keep going back over the same spot because it still looks wrong, even if it’s probably fine.\n\nSorry—what amount do you mean? Like a whole meal, or more than that?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “lost control.” That sounds so… dramatic, like you’re not aware of what you’re doing.\n\nSometimes I’m eating and I feel uneasy, like I should stop or I shouldn’t have started, but then I get more caught up in the feeling afterward than the eating itself. I’ll start thinking about whether I need to clean the counters again, or if I left streaks on the floor at work—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. What do you mean by lost control—like not being able to stop at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without being able to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had it like that, where it’s a really large amount and I truly can’t stop.\n\nSometimes if I eat more than I meant to, it feels wrong afterward, and then I get stuck thinking about it. But “large amount in a short period”… I don’t know. My sense of what’s large might not be the same as yours.\n\nAt work, sometimes on break I’ll take a few bites of something and then keep thinking about whether I should’ve had it at all, and then I’m thinking about the floors or the supply closet being out of order—sorry. I guess I’m saying I don’t really know how to answer that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like on purpose, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes after eating I feel really uncomfortable and I get stuck thinking about it, but making myself vomit—no, I can’t say that’s something I do. I mean, I’ve felt nauseated before, but that’s different, isn’t it? Like if something doesn’t sit right, or if the trash room at work smells bad after the weekend and then everything feels off.\n\nSorry, I’m getting away from it. No, I don’t make myself vomit after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to make up for something you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I do move around a lot at work, but that’s just part of cleaning. There are stairs, hallways, carrying supplies, and if someone leaves mud tracked in then it’s twice as much mopping, especially by the side entrance.\n\nBut exercising to make up for eating— I don’t really go to a gym or anything like that. I might feel uneasy after I eat, but I’m not sure I’d call what I do “exercise.” I guess I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I… yes, maybe. I don’t always trust what I’m seeing, I guess.\n\nIf I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full, it feels like I’ve let myself go, like I’m not keeping things under control. But if I stay small and neat, then I feel like I’m handling everything right.\n\nThat sounds silly when I say it out loud. And mirrors in bathrooms are strange anyway—the lighting at work makes everything look worse, especially the one near the back hallway where the bulbs flicker. But… yes, I think I probably see it differently than other people do.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to control your eating?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I mean, I don’t like saying it that way, but it is probably the main thing.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, then afterward I spend the rest of the day worrying it’s going to show on me somehow. I’ll keep checking myself, or thinking I should make up for it somehow.\n\nBiscuits are funny, too, because at work someone leaves them in the break room sometimes, and they just sit there getting stale under that plastic lid—but if I have one, then I can’t stop thinking about it. So… yes, I think the fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I try to control what I eat.\n\nDoctor: Is your attempt to control your eating mostly about feeling in control of your life, or is it mostly about not gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly not gaining weight, I think. Feeling in control is part of it, I suppose, but it’s more that if I eat something heavier—like biscuits, or pasta—I spend the rest of the day scared it’s going to show on me somehow.\n\nThen I start checking myself, and thinking I need to make up for it. It’s silly, because one biscuit isn’t… well, I know what people would say. At work they leave them in the break room sometimes, and the plastic lid never fits right, so they get soft around the edges—but if I have one, it stays in my head all day. So yes, mostly the weight part.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly eat much more food than most people would in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not “much more,” anyway. I’m trying to think what you’d count as a short period of time.\n\nSometimes if I’m cleaning the staff room late and there are leftovers out, I’ll notice them, but I don’t usually sit down and eat a lot all at once. The night crew leaves takeaway boxes sometimes, and they smell awful by the morning if nobody throws them out—especially curry, it gets into the bins.\n\nBut, um… no, I haven’t really noticed myself eating huge amounts like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your attempt to restrict your food intake mostly about preventing weight gain or about making up for times when you feel you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about preventing weight gain, I think. The “making up for it” part comes after, if that makes sense.\n\nLike if I eat something heavier—biscuits or pasta or something like that—I spend the rest of the day worrying it’s going to show on me. I’ll keep sort of checking myself, and then I start thinking I should make up for it somehow.\n\nBiscuits at work are the worst because they’re just sitting there in the break room, and people act like it’s nothing, just have one with tea, but for me it stays in my head. So… yes, it’s more the fear of gaining weight. The making up is because of that.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal when restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s the main thing. It’s not just “being good” or whatever people say. It’s that if I eat something heavier, like pasta or biscuits, I can’t stop thinking it’s going to show on me somehow.\n\nI’ll catch myself checking, you know, my clothes, my stomach… and then I start thinking I need to make up for it. Even if it was just something small. It gets stuck in my head the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you knew you were at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, “healthy” sounds sensible when you say it like that, but in my head it doesn’t really stop the worry.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I can spend the whole rest of the day thinking it’s going to show on me. I’ll check my clothes, my stomach, just sort of… looking for proof. And then I start thinking I need to make up for it.\n\nIt’s silly, because people at work will have biscuits and then just carry on mopping or chatting about the rota, and I’m still thinking about it hours later. So yes, even if someone told me my weight was healthy, I think I’d still be frightened of gaining.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would see yourself as fat even if you were at a weight that most people would consider healthy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I might. Not all the time, maybe, but if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little bit full, I just feel… wrong. Like I’ve let myself go.\n\nIt sounds daft, because I can be on my feet all day cleaning, and nobody else is looking that closely—well, I don’t suppose they are—but I still notice it. If I feel small and tidy, neat, then I feel like I’m managing things properly. Like I’ve got myself under control.\n\nBut if I look fuller, even just after eating, my mind goes straight to, “You’ve got fat.” So yes… even if other people said the weight was healthy, I don’t know that I’d see it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I’ll keep checking myself, or thinking I should make up for it somehow.\n\nBiscuits are funny, too, because at work someone leaves them in the break room sometimes, and they just sit there getting stale under that plastic lid—but if I have one, then I can’t stop thinking about it. So… yes, I think the fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I try to control what I eat.\n\nDoctor: Is your attempt to control your eating mostly about feeling in control of your life, or is it mostly about not gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly not gaining weight, I think. Feeling in control is part of it, I suppose, but it’s more that if I eat something heavier—like biscuits, or pasta—I spend the rest of the day scared it’s going to show on me somehow.\n\nThen I start checking myself, and thinking I need to make up for it. It’s silly, because one biscuit isn’t… well, I know what people would say. At work they leave them in the break room sometimes, and the plastic lid never fits right, so they get soft around the edges—but if I have one, it stays in my head all day. So yes, mostly the weight part.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly eat much more food than most people would in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not “much more,” anyway. I’m trying to think what you’d count as a short period of time.\n\nSometimes if I’m cleaning the staff room late and there are leftovers out, I’ll notice them, but I don’t usually sit down and eat a lot all at once. The night crew leaves takeaway boxes sometimes, and they smell awful by the morning if nobody throws them out—especially curry, it gets into the bins.\n\nBut, um… no, I haven’t really noticed myself eating huge amounts like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your attempt to restrict your food intake mostly about preventing weight gain or about making up for times when you feel you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about preventing weight gain, I think. The “making up for it” part comes after, if that makes sense.\n\nLike if I eat something heavier—biscuits or pasta or something like that—I spend the rest of the day worrying it’s going to show on me. I’ll keep sort of checking myself, and then I start thinking I should make up for it somehow.\n\nBiscuits at work are the worst because they’re just sitting there in the break room, and people act like it’s nothing, just have one with tea, but for me it stays in my head. So yes, even if someone told me my weight was healthy, I think I’d still be frightened of gaining.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would see yourself as fat even if you were at a weight that most people would consider healthy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I might. Not all the time, maybe, but if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little bit full, I just feel… wrong. Like I’ve let myself go.\n\nIt sounds daft, because I can be on my feet all day cleaning, and nobody else is looking that closely—well, I don’t suppose they are—but I still notice it. Like I’ve got myself under control.\n\nBut if I look fuller, even just after eating, my mind goes straight to, “You’ve got fat.” So yes… even if other people said the weight was healthy, I don’t know that I’d see it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you look, rather than just the number on the scale?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is more how I look, or how I think I look. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. Not attractive exactly like—well, I’m not trying to look glamorous at my age, you know, I’m usually in work trousers and an old fleece, and half the time there’s bleach on my sleeve. But if I look bigger, especially round my stomach, I just feel awful about myself.

When I stay small and neat, I feel like I’m handling everything right. Like things are under control. But if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I think, “Oh, you’ve let yourself go.” It makes me feel untidy, I suppose… not right. So yes, I think the fear is tied up with thinking I’d look… wrong, or unattractive, if I gained.

---------- turn 19 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… my doctor kind of pushed me to come. She says I’ve gotten too thin. I mean, I guess I can see it—my work pants are hanging loose on me, and I’m always tugging them up when I’m cleaning, which is annoying because you can’t be fussing with that when you’re mopping floors and trying not to slip.  \n\nBut… I don’t know. Part of me still feels relieved when the number on the scale is lower, even though people keep acting worried about it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose I am. I don’t like saying it that plain, but if I eat something heavier—like biscuits, or pasta, you know, the kind of thing that sits in your stomach—I spend the rest of the day thinking it’s going to show on me somehow. Like I’ll catch myself checking my waist or how my shirt hangs.\n\nIt’s silly, because one meal shouldn’t do all that, I know, but then I get it in my head that I need to make up for it. And then I’m distracted all day, even at work, which is not helpful when someone’s left muddy footprints through the hallway again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I see the number the same way other people do. They say I’m too thin, but in my mind, if I stay small and… tidy-looking, I guess, it feels like I’m keeping things under control.\n\nBut then if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full, I get this awful feeling like I’ve let myself go. Like I’ve failed somehow. Which sounds dramatic, I know. It’s just—when you’re cleaning all day, you notice every little smudge and bit of dust, and I think I do that with myself too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight or body shape every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think. It sort of sneaks in while I’m doing other things. Like I’ll be wiping down a sink or straightening a trash liner and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether my waistband feels different.\n\nWhen I stay small and neat, it feels like I’m handling everything the right way. But if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just… I feel like I’ve let myself go. Even if nobody else would notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what would count as excessive, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel like I ate too much even if it’s just… more than I planned, or something that feels heavy.\n\nBut a large amount in a short time? I don’t know. I don’t think of it that way. I tend to remember more how it feels afterward, like whether I feel too full or uncomfortable. And then I get distracted thinking about it, like when I’m mopping and I keep going back over the same spot because it still looks wrong, even if it’s probably fine.\n\nSorry—what amount do you mean? Like a whole meal, or more than that?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “lost control.” That sounds so… dramatic, like you’re not aware of what you’re doing.\n\nSometimes I’m eating and I feel uneasy, like I should stop or I shouldn’t have started, but then I get more caught up in the feeling afterward than the eating itself. I’ll start thinking about whether I need to clean the counters again, or if I left streaks on the floor at work—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. What do you mean by lost control—like not being able to stop at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without being able to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had it like that, where it’s a really large amount and I truly can’t stop.\n\nSometimes if I eat more than I meant to, it feels wrong afterward, and then I get stuck thinking about it. But “large amount in a short period”… I don’t know. My sense of what’s large might not be the same as yours.\n\nAt work, sometimes on break I’ll take a few bites of something and then keep thinking about whether I should’ve had it at all, and then I’m thinking about the floors or the supply closet being out of order—sorry. I guess I’m saying I don’t really know how to answer that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like on purpose, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes after eating I feel really uncomfortable and I get stuck thinking about it, but making myself vomit—no, I can’t say that’s something I do. I mean, I’ve felt nauseated before, but that’s different, isn’t it? Like if something doesn’t sit right, or if the trash room at work smells bad after the weekend and then everything feels off.\n\nSorry, I’m getting away from it. No, I don’t make myself vomit after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to make up for something you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I do move around a lot at work, but that’s just part of cleaning. There are stairs, hallways, carrying supplies, and if someone leaves mud tracked in then it’s twice as much mopping, especially by the side entrance.\n\nBut exercising to make up for eating— I don’t really go to a gym or anything like that. I might feel uneasy after I eat, but I’m not sure I’d call what I do “exercise.” I guess I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I… yes, maybe. I don’t always trust what I’m seeing, I guess.\n\nIf I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full, it feels like I’ve let myself go, like I’m not keeping things under control. But if I stay small and neat, then I feel like I’m handling everything right.\n\nThat sounds silly when I say it out loud. And mirrors in bathrooms are strange anyway—the lighting at work makes everything look worse, especially the one near the back hallway where the bulbs flicker. But… yes, I think I probably see it differently than other people do.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to control your eating?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I mean, I don’t like saying it that way, but it is probably the main thing.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, then afterward I spend the rest of the day worrying it’s going to show on me somehow. I’ll keep checking myself, or thinking I should make up for it somehow.\n\nBiscuits are funny, too, because at work someone leaves them in the break room sometimes, and they just sit there getting stale under that plastic lid—but if I have one, then I can’t stop thinking about it. So… yes, I think the fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I try to control what I eat.\n\nDoctor: Is your attempt to control your eating mostly about feeling in control of your life, or is it mostly about not gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly not gaining weight, I think. Feeling in control is part of it, I suppose, but it’s more that if I eat something heavier—like biscuits, or pasta—I spend the rest of the day scared it’s going to show on me somehow.\n\nThen I start checking myself, and thinking I need to make up for it. It’s silly, because one biscuit isn’t… well, I know what people would say. At work they leave them in the break room sometimes, and the plastic lid never fits right, so they get soft around the edges—but if I have one, it stays in my head all day. So yes, mostly the weight part.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly eat much more food than most people would in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not “much more,” anyway. I’m trying to think what you’d count as a short period of time.\n\nSometimes if I’m cleaning the staff room late and there are leftovers out, I’ll notice them, but I don’t usually sit down and eat a lot all at once. The night crew leaves takeaway boxes sometimes, and they smell awful by the morning if nobody throws them out—especially curry, it gets into the bins.\n\nBut, um… no, I haven’t really noticed myself eating huge amounts like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your attempt to restrict your food intake mostly about preventing weight gain or about making up for times when you feel you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about preventing weight gain, I think. The “making up for it” part comes after, if that makes sense.\n\nLike if I eat something heavier—biscuits or pasta or something like that—I spend the rest of the day worrying it’s going to show on me. I’ll keep sort of checking myself, and then I start thinking I should make up for it somehow.\n\nBiscuits at work are the worst because they’re just sitting there in the break room, and people act like it’s nothing, just have one with tea, but for me it stays in my head. So… yes, it’s more the fear of gaining weight. The making up is because of that.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal when restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s the main thing. It’s not just “being good” or whatever people say. It’s that if I eat something heavier, like pasta or biscuits, I can’t stop thinking it’s going to show on me somehow.\n\nI’ll catch myself checking, you know, my clothes, my stomach… and then I start thinking I need to make up for it. Even if it was just something small. It gets stuck in my head the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you knew you were at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, “healthy” sounds sensible when you say it like that, but in my head it doesn’t really stop the worry.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I can spend the whole rest of the day thinking it’s going to show on me. I’ll check my clothes, my stomach, just sort of… looking for proof. And then I start thinking I need to make up for it.\n\nIt’s silly, because people at work will have biscuits and then just carry on mopping or chatting about the rota, and I’m still thinking about it hours later. So yes, even if someone told me my weight was healthy, I think I’d still be frightened of gaining.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would see yourself as fat even if you were at a weight that most people would consider healthy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I might. Not all the time, maybe, but if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little bit full, I just feel… wrong. Like I’ve let myself go.\n\nIt sounds daft, because I can be on my feet all day cleaning, and nobody else is looking that closely—well, I don’t suppose they are—but I still notice it. If I feel small and tidy, neat, then I feel like I’m managing things properly. Like I’ve got myself under control.\n\nBut if I look fuller, even just after eating, my mind goes straight to, “You’ve got fat.” So yes… even if other people said the weight was healthy, I don’t know that I’d see it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you look, rather than just the number on the scale?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is more how I look, or how I think I look. The number matters, but if I look in the mirror and my stomach seems even a bit full, I feel like I’ve let myself go.\n\nWhen I feel small and neat, everything feels… in order, like I’m managing properly. Like when I’ve done a corridor at work and the floor’s all clean and you can see where you’ve been—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But that’s how it feels in my body too. If I stay small, I feel like I’m handling things right. If I look fuller, I just feel wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not attractive exactly like—well, I’m not trying to look glamorous at my age, you know, I’m usually in work trousers and an old fleece, and half the time there’s bleach on my sleeve. But if I look bigger, especially round my stomach, I just feel awful about myself.\n\nWhen I stay small and neat, I feel like I’m handling everything right. Like things are under control. But if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I think, “Oh, you’ve let yourself go.” It makes me feel untidy, I suppose… not right. So yes, I think the fear is tied up with thinking I’d look… wrong, or unattractive, if I gained.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… my doctor kind of pushed me to come. She says I’ve gotten too thin. I mean, I guess I can see it—my work pants are hanging loose on me, and I’m always tugging them up when I’m cleaning, which is annoying because you can’t be fussing with that when you’re mopping floors and trying not to slip.  \n\nBut… I don’t know. Part of me still feels relieved when the number on the scale is lower, even though people keep acting worried about it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose I am. I don’t like saying it that plain, but if I eat something heavier—like biscuits, or pasta, you know, the kind of thing that sits in your stomach—I spend the rest of the day thinking it’s going to show on me somehow. Like I’ll catch myself checking my waist or how my shirt hangs.\n\nIt’s silly, because one meal shouldn’t do all that, I know, but then I get it in my head that I need to make up for it. And then I’m distracted all day, even at work, which is not helpful when someone’s left muddy footprints through the hallway again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I see the number the same way other people do. They say I’m too thin, but in my mind, if I stay small and… tidy-looking, I guess, it feels like I’m keeping things under control.\n\nBut then if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full, I get this awful feeling like I’ve let myself go. Like I’ve failed somehow. Which sounds dramatic, I know. It’s just—when you’re cleaning all day, you notice every little smudge and bit of dust, and I think I do that with myself too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight or body shape every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think. It sort of sneaks in while I’m doing other things. Like I’ll be wiping down a sink or straightening a trash liner and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether my waistband feels different.\n\nWhen I stay small and neat, it feels like I’m handling everything the right way. But if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just… I feel like I’ve let myself go. Even if nobody else would notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what would count as excessive, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel like I ate too much even if it’s just… more than I planned, or something that feels heavy.\n\nBut a large amount in a short time? I don’t know. I don’t think of it that way. I tend to remember more how it feels afterward, like whether I feel too full or uncomfortable. Like a whole meal, or more than that?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “lost control.” That sounds so… dramatic, like you’re not aware of what you’re doing.\n\nSometimes I’m eating and I feel uneasy, like I should stop or I shouldn’t have started, but then I get more caught up in the feeling afterward than the eating itself. What do you mean by lost control—like not being able to stop at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without being able to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had it like that, where it’s a really large amount and I truly can’t stop.\n\nSometimes if I eat more than I meant to, it feels wrong afterward, and then I get stuck thinking about it. But “large amount in a short period”… I don’t know. My sense of what’s large might not be the same as yours.\n\nAt work, sometimes on break I’ll take a few bites of something and then keep thinking about whether I should’ve had it at all, and then I’m thinking about the floors or the supply closet being out of order—sorry. I guess I’m saying I don’t really know how to answer that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like on purpose, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes after eating I feel really uncomfortable and I get stuck thinking about it, but making myself vomit—no, I can’t say that’s something I do. I mean, I’ve felt nauseated before, but that’s different, isn’t it? Like if something doesn’t sit right, or if the trash room at work smells bad after the weekend and then everything feels off.\n\nSorry, I’m getting away from it. No, I don’t make myself vomit after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to make up for something you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I do move around a lot at work, but that’s just part of cleaning. There are stairs, hallways, carrying supplies, and if someone leaves mud tracked in then it’s twice as much mopping, especially by the side entrance.\n\nBut exercising to make up for eating— I don’t really go to a gym or anything like that. I might feel uneasy after I eat, but I’m not sure I’d call what I do “exercise.” I guess I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I… yes, maybe. I don’t always trust what I’m seeing, I guess.\n\nIf I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full, it feels like I’ve let myself go, like I’m not keeping things under control. But if I stay small and neat, then I feel like I’m handling everything right.\n\nThat sounds silly when I say it out loud. And mirrors in bathrooms are strange anyway—the lighting at work makes everything look worse, especially the one near the back hallway where the bulbs flicker. But… yes, I think I probably see it differently than other people do.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to control your eating?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I mean, I don’t like saying it that way, but it is probably the main thing.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, then afterward I spend the rest of the day worrying it’s going to show on me somehow. I’ll keep checking myself, or thinking I should make up for it somehow.\n\nBiscuits are funny, too, because at work someone leaves them in the break room sometimes, and they just sit there getting stale under that plastic lid—but if I have one, then I can’t stop thinking about it. So… yes, I think the fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I try to control what I eat.\n\nDoctor: Is your attempt to control your eating mostly about feeling in control of your life, or is it mostly about not gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly not gaining weight, I think. Feeling in control is part of it, I suppose, but it’s more that if I eat something heavier—like biscuits, or pasta—I spend the rest of the day scared it’s going to show on me somehow.\n\nThen I start checking myself, and thinking I need to make up for it. It’s silly, because one biscuit isn’t… well, I know what people would say. At work they leave them in the break room sometimes, and the plastic lid never fits right, so they get soft around the edges—but if I have one, it stays in my head all day. So yes, mostly the weight part.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly eat much more food than most people would in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not “much more,” anyway. I’m trying to think what you’d count as a short period of time.\n\nSometimes if I’m cleaning the staff room late and there are leftovers out, I’ll notice them, but I don’t usually sit down and eat a lot all at once. The night crew leaves takeaway boxes sometimes, and they smell awful by the morning if nobody throws them out—especially curry, it gets into the bins.\n\nBut, um… no, I haven’t really noticed myself eating huge amounts like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your attempt to restrict your food intake mostly about preventing weight gain or about making up for times when you feel you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about preventing weight gain, I think. The “making up for it” part comes after, if that makes sense.\n\nLike if I eat something heavier—biscuits or pasta or something like that—I spend the rest of the day worrying it’s going to show on me. I’ll keep sort of checking myself, and then I start thinking I should make up for it somehow.\n\nBiscuits at work are the worst because they’re just sitting there in the break room, and people act like it’s nothing, just have one with tea, but for me it stays in my head. So… yes, it’s more the fear of gaining weight. The making up is because of that.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal when restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s the main thing. It’s not just “being good” or whatever people say. It’s that if I eat something heavier, like pasta or biscuits, I can’t stop thinking it’s going to show on me somehow.\n\nI’ll catch myself checking, you know, my clothes, my stomach… and then I start thinking I need to make up for it. Even if it was just something small. It gets stuck in my head the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you knew you were at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, “healthy” sounds sensible when you say it like that, but in my head it doesn’t really stop the worry.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I can spend the whole rest of the day thinking it’s going to show on me. I’ll check my clothes, my stomach, just sort of… looking for proof. And then I start thinking I need to make up for it.\n\nIt’s silly, because people at work will have biscuits and then just carry on mopping or chatting about the rota, and I’m still thinking about it hours later. So yes, even if someone told me my weight was healthy, I think I’d still be frightened of gaining.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would see yourself as fat even if you were at a weight that most people would consider healthy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I might. Not all the time, maybe, but if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little bit full, I just feel… wrong. Like I’ve let myself go.\n\nIt sounds daft, because I can be on my feet all day cleaning, and nobody else is looking that closely—well, I don’t suppose they are—but I still notice it. If I feel small and tidy, neat, then I feel like I’m managing things properly. Like I’ve got myself under control.\n\nBut if I look fuller, even just after eating, my mind goes straight to, “You’ve got fat.” So yes… even if other people said the weight was healthy, I don’t know that I’d see it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you look, rather than just the number on the scale?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is more how I look, or how I think I look. The number matters, but if I look in the mirror and my stomach seems even a bit full, I feel like I’ve let myself go.\n\nWhen I feel small and neat, everything feels… in order, like I’m managing properly. Like when I’ve done a corridor at work and the floor’s all clean and you can see where you’ve been—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But that’s how it feels in my body too. If I stay small, I feel like I’m handling things right. If I look fuller, I just feel wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not attractive exactly like—well, I’m not trying to look glamorous at my age, you know, I’m usually in work trousers and an old fleece, and half the time there’s bleach on my sleeve. But if I look bigger, especially round my stomach, I just feel awful about myself.\n\nWhen I stay small and neat, I feel like I’m handling everything right. Like things are under control. But if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I think, “Oh, you’ve let yourself go.” It makes me feel untidy, I suppose… not right. So yes, I think the fear is tied up with thinking I’d look… wrong, or unattractive, if I gained.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you have a distorted view of what you look like?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it distorted… I mean, I know mirrors can be funny, the ones in the staff changing room are dreadful, and the lighting makes everyone look half-dead. I’ve said that to the girls before, though they just laugh and get on with it.\n\nBut yes, I suppose maybe I don’t see myself the way other people do. When I stay small and neat, I feel like I’m handling everything right, like I’ve kept things in order. But if my stomach looks even the least bit full in the mirror, I feel like I’ve let myself go. It just hits me straight away, even if nothing has really changed."
